<a id="msg_002d501229a03492016ad52eb51d9c8191a43238c1cda71faf"></a>

## 2026-10-18T20:41:50.904Z · assistant · final_answer

The current build needs substantial improvement in its **main shapes, anatomy, and integration with the cabinet**. Its solid geometry provides a useful foundation, but the dragon still reads as a collection of separately designed parts: a tapered column of scales, a rounded head, a curved green panel, and a conventional shelf grid.

The stronger references make those parts appear to belong to one creature. A shoulder explains where a wing begins. A broad chest narrows into a neck. Scales change size as the anatomy changes. The cabinet’s borders follow those movements.

The earlier checks established closed meshes and tested particular clearances. They did **not** establish convincing anatomy, faithful proportions, or convincing carving. These images expose that distinction clearly.

I’m comparing the supplied references with the current default build and its close-up screenshots. Dimensions, hidden supports, and fabrication methods cannot be established reliably from these images alone.

**1. Image 1 — The carved, single-wing cabinet is the most relevant reference for the current design.**

Its defining feature is the relationship between the **swollen lower body, narrowing neck, and aggressive outward-projecting head**. The right side resembles a rounded haunch or pear-shaped torso. Its outside contour pushes outward near the lower shelves, then pulls inward beneath the head. That change in width gives the creature weight.

The current body is comparatively straight. Although it tapers, it has little sense of a chest, shoulder, throat, or haunch. Its scales reinforce the vertical direction, making it resemble a decorated post. The reference’s body has a more expressive sequence: broad mass, inward bend, narrow neck, then a sudden outward movement into the muzzle.

The head is also much more articulated. Its brow, cheek plates, nasal ridge, horn roots, jaw hinge, and beard create overlapping planes. The eye sits within that architecture. Our large exposed eye and smooth rounded skull produce a friendlier, more toy-like expression.

Notice how the scales change. Broad, overlapping plates cover the lower body; smaller, tighter plates climb the neck. They turn around the anatomy rather than merely repeating around an upright axis. The broad plates resemble carved leaves with substantial shoulders and softened, irregular points.

The wing balances this dense body with a comparatively quiet green surface. Its timber ribs provide long lines that the eye can follow. The carved inner border then connects that sweep to the shelving.

The shelving remains recognisable furniture: relatively disciplined bays, usable horizontal boards, and a continuous base. The claw-like junctions are small accents. Their job is to suggest that the creature holds the cabinet together.

The main risk of overbuilding here is adding equal ornament everywhere. The head already supplies a dense focal point; the wing needs its broad green areas, and the shelves need enough calm space for books to remain legible.

**What this contributes:** a much stronger body silhouette, concentrated facial detail, changing scale sizes, and a flowing boundary between anatomy and furniture.

---

**2. Image 2 — The two-wing domestic bookcase gives the dragon a relationship with its user.**

The central dragon divides two large shelving wings. Its head inclines towards the person, which changes its character: it feels attentive and watchful rather than like a crest fixed above a cabinet.

There is a clear distinction between the broad plates running down the throat and belly and the smaller scales along the sides. That distinction is missing from the current build. A continuous covering of similar teardrop plates gives every surface the same identity.

The wing outlines bulge around the main storage areas and tighten towards their curled tips. Inside those outlines, the horizontal shelves remain comparatively regular. This is a useful compromise between expressive silhouette and practical book storage.

The person also reveals functional questions that isolated furniture renders conceal. A reader must approach the shelves, stand comfortably, reach between projections, and withdraw a book past the dragon’s chest. Book clearance alone does not establish hand or forearm clearance.

The low tail-like nest, small perch carrying a mug, large feet, and central body add personality. They also complicate the floor area. Each additional use creates another surface to clean, another obstruction, or another possible spill location.

The image does not prove how those wings are supported. The visible human figure helps communicate intended scale, but it should not be used as a reliable measuring instrument.

**What this contributes:** a readable chest, a responsive head pose, practical shelving inside expressive wings, and attention to how someone actually uses the piece.

---

**3. Image 3 — The circular wall composition is strongest in movement and negative space.**

Here, the dragon’s body supplies the central movement of the composition. It bends across the shelf grid, while the wing edges and tail establish a large enclosing circle.

The straight shelves act as a stable background. Against them, the body’s bends become conspicuous. Our current build has regular shelves beside a mostly upright body, so the contrast between creature and cabinet is much weaker.

The tail is especially important. It carries the movement downwards and then along the bottom, giving the dragon an apparent continuation beyond its torso. The current body ends abruptly at the plinth. That can work as a carved furniture side, but it needs a deliberately designed termination.

This reference also uses different densities of detail. Larger body masses remain readable underneath the smaller scales and spines. Those small elements follow the body’s direction, strengthening its bend.

The amber shelf lighting separates the books from the dark foreground creature. That is effective presentation, but it also helps conceal how much storage may be obstructed behind the dragon. A shelf can be visible through a gap while still being difficult to use.

Its main overbuild risk is visual density: dark scales, spikes, wings, tail, illuminated shelves, and decorative objects all compete within a compact outline.

**What this contributes:** a convincing body gesture, an enclosing silhouette, and deliberate empty spaces between anatomy and shelves.

---

**4. Image 4 — The reading pavilion demonstrates architectural depth and bodily presence.**

This is a reading environment with a dragon forming its enclosure. The chair establishes a place for a person; the wing and shelving rise around that occupation.

The neck has a substantial bend. Its broad underside plates follow the throat, while the smaller side scales turn around the neck. The head extends from that bend with a believable sense of projection and weight.

The wings have different roles. The left behaves primarily as an enclosing membrane. The right becomes a shelving wall. That asymmetry is useful: both sides belong to the same creature, while each serves a different spatial purpose.

The shelving also appears to wrap through depth. Its compartments do not all sit on one flat presentation plane. That makes the assembly feel more architectural than our current comparatively flat wing surround.

The tail creates a foreground boundary around the chair. It strengthens the composition, but it also narrows the route into the reading area. The platform introduces a step, and the projecting head occupies space above the seated user.

Those features need to be evaluated as occupied space. A comfortable-looking chair does not automatically make the surrounding enclosure comfortable.

**What this contributes:** meaningful depth, a clear neck-and-chest relationship, and a design organised around a person’s activity. The platform and room-scale wings belong to this larger brief; they do not need to be transferred into our cabinet.

---

**5. Image 5 — The illuminated pavilion shows both effective emphasis and excessive stimulation.**

The pale, angular throat plates form a clear central rhythm. Their chevron-like arrangement is very different from the fine scales surrounding them. This gives the underside of the neck an identity of its own.

The left wing’s warm translucent appearance separates membrane from frame. The shelving wing uses illumination to reveal compartment depth and distinguish front edges from recessed backs.

However, the eyes, mouth, throat, wing, shelves, and platform are all strongly illuminated. The result has several competing focal points. The base lighting is particularly conspicuous because it creates a bright, repetitive fringe around the entire platform.

There is a similar issue with the shelf contents. Many compartments contain small display objects alongside books. That can suit a cabinet of curiosities, but it changes the purpose from book storage towards an illuminated collection display.

The current build would benefit from better material separation and more controlled lighting. It does not need every expressive feature to glow. Its anatomy needs to remain readable in daylight and clay mode.

**What this contributes:** clear separation between membrane, frame, belly plates, and shelving. Its caution is that lighting and ornament can overwhelm the furniture’s principal use.

---

**6. Image 6 — The dragon holding a stepped cabinet has the clearest separation between creature and furniture.**

This dragon appears to stand beside and hold a cabinet. Its shoulder, arm, hand, chest, leg, foot, and tail establish a recognisable bodily arrangement.

That gives the cabinet an understandable relationship with the creature. The hand actually reaches towards its contents. The foot has a planted contact with the base. The tail continues around the side instead of disappearing into an undifferentiated column.

The lowered head and mostly closed mouth give it a thoughtful, less theatrical character. Its facial expression comes from the brow, muzzle, cheek shapes, and head angle.

Its surface hierarchy is particularly useful. Broad pale belly plates contrast with fine, dark, pebbled scales. Larger forms explain the anatomy before the tiny surface pattern becomes visible.

The cabinet itself is comparatively straightforward timber joinery. Its stepped cubbies suit a mixture of books and display objects. This contrast helps both the creature and the furniture remain understandable.

There are practical trade-offs. Large claws can obstruct low compartments; a gripping hand may interfere with book removal; numerous small cubbies restrict flexibility. The visible metal-like fixing detail at the foot also introduces a construction language that would need to be coordinated elsewhere.

**What this contributes:** articulated anatomy, convincing contact gestures, differentiated surface regions, and a simpler cabinet that allows the sculpture to carry the identity.

---

**7. Image 7 — The wings genuinely participate in the shelf design.**

The shelves occupy the wings’ spread structure. They follow the available spaces between the wing borders and supporting forms. The scalloped lower edges preserve the bat-wing identity.

This is a stronger integration than placing a normal cabinet next to a wing-shaped panel. The wing remains recognisable because of its leading edge, finger-like divisions, membrane folds, and scalloped outline—even though much of its interior accommodates books.

The seated dragon has substantial thighs, knees, hands, and feet. These masses establish a pose. The tail moves down beside the lower cabinet, visually connecting the upper sculpture to the floor-level furniture.

The relatively consistent timber appearance also helps. Recognition depends on shape and carving rather than contrasting colours or dramatic illumination.

Its principal overbuild is the enormous spread. Those high, extended storage wings would need a convincing support strategy. The ladder and the books on the floor also indicate that the design has exceeded ordinary, easy-reach storage.

Some book arrangements depend on angled surfaces and neighbouring books. A buildable interpretation would need clear retaining edges and stable support at each compartment.

**What this contributes:** shelving derived from wing structure, convincing seated body mass, and strong recognition through silhouette alone.

---

**8. Image 8 — The standing dragon and sail-like wing has the most distinctive economy of silhouette.**

One dominant wing rises above a comparatively slender standing creature. The pointed outer sweep, diagonal divisions, and large empty spaces give it a clear directional movement.

The legs show an intelligible chain from hip to knee to ankle and planted toes. The body therefore has an apparent route for its weight to reach the floor. The tail extends behind it and helps balance the composition, although actual stability cannot be established from the image.

The large carved curves around the shoulder and haunch are important. They describe substantial masses. They are much more effective than covering every part with the same small scale pattern.

The wing compartments vary with the tapering outline. Their diagonals belong to an overall fan-like arrangement rather than looking like arbitrary tilted shelves.

The hands become a small tray for books and a mug. That makes the creature’s pose useful and gives a person an obvious point of interaction.

There are functional tensions: a hanging plant loads the far wing tip and introduces water above books; drinks share space with paper; angled book compartments need dependable retaining surfaces. The narrow floor footprint also needs an explicit support and anchoring solution.

**What this contributes:** a strong primary sweep, articulated support through the legs, purposeful large curves, and shelves organised by the wing’s geometry.

---

Comparing these references directly with our build, I would make the following changes.

**The body needs a sculptural redesign.** Its current upright, slightly tapering form gives the scales too much responsibility for creating the dragon. The underlying volume should already suggest the creature before any scales are added.

For the existing single-wing cabinet, that means a fuller lower body, a more definite inward bend beneath the neck, a visible throat direction, and a deliberate transition into the base. Image 1 supplies the most appropriate model. Adding full legs would change this particular furniture concept; a well-resolved carved body-side could work without them.

**The head needs stronger planes and relationships.** The present rounded skull, prominent eye, smooth muzzle, and long open mouth produce a cartoon-like character. More polygons on those same shapes will preserve that character.

The brow should emerge from a bony roof over the eye. The cheek needs a defined mass behind the mouth. The muzzle needs a nasal bridge and a clearer change of section towards the nostrils. The lower jaw should widen towards its hinge, then taper towards the chin. Horns should emerge from shaped root masses and follow a coordinated direction.

The eye should be nested within those forms. The current raised pupil and separate curved brow are particularly conspicuous in close-up. The references generally achieve a more powerful expression with a smaller visible eye surrounded by stronger anatomy.

**The wing needs a readable origin and a fan-like structure.** Our long, nearly parallel ribs make it resemble a curved screen or decorative canopy. The references suggest a joint, a strong leading edge, and fingers spreading into the wing.

The green areas also need more variation in camber and tension. Between supporting fingers, a membrane can tighten, sag slightly, or draw into a scalloped edge. A uniformly curved infill gives every panel the same behaviour.

The solid thickness should remain. The improvement is in its cross-sections and curvature: broader near an attachment, thinner towards an edge, with transitions that explain how one part meets another.

**The scales need anatomical regions.** The current repetition is too easy to detect. Similar elongated plates repeat around the body with broadly similar orientation.

I would distinguish broad belly or throat plates, medium overlapping flank scales, smaller neck scales, and a quieter treatment at the rear or within tight transitions. Scale size, width, curvature, and direction should respond to the underlying mass.

Variation should follow those regions. Randomly rotating identical scales would add visual noise without explaining the creature.

**The carving needs to look carved.** The close-up shelf borders currently resemble raised cords tracing sine waves. They have physical depth, but they do not yet resemble the broad, flattened, undercut ribbons and leaves visible in the stronger carved references.

A better motif would have shoulders, a changing section, a shallow recessed background, and deliberate terminations. It should broaden where the border broadens and resolve cleanly at a corner or junction.

The same applies to the claw brackets. Three similar rounded fingers on a rectangular palm plate read as applied tabs. A grip needs unequal finger lengths, knuckles, changing pressure, and a direction around the shelf edge. A few convincing grips would contribute more than the same simplified motif repeated at every available junction.

**The cabinet’s upper outline needs cleaner resolution.** The current central arch, tall uprights, curled bosses, wing border, and right-hand surround create several competing endings.

Each upper member needs a clear destination: another frame member, a wing joint, a carved finial, or an intentional free tip. Small scrolls should complete that geometry rather than act as punctuation attached wherever a gap remains.

The main shelving can stay disciplined. The strongest reference for our cabinet preserves practical storage inside a sculptural surround.

**The base needs a more distinctive profile.** Its broad cap and stacked rectangular layers currently read as a generic display plinth. The scroll feet are small relative to the whole composition.

The improvement should come from the base’s section, corner treatment, contact with the body, and visible foot shapes. Adding further moulding layers would increase complexity without establishing a stronger furniture identity.

**The materials need a clearer construction story.** The smooth brown finish currently unifies the object, but it also makes the rounded anatomy look somewhat plastic.

A carved timber interpretation should distinguish broad cut surfaces, polished edges, recessed carving, end grain, and assembled boards. Grain direction should support the chosen fabrication story. A head carved from a large block and a membrane panel fitted into a frame should not receive identical-looking grain treatment.

Images 1 and 7 offer useful guidance here. Their timber appearance works with the larger shapes. Images 4 and 5 demonstrate dramatic finishes, but their lighting should not become a substitute for resolved form.

**The books and usable spaces need more deliberate composition.** The current book construction is substantially better than a collection of plain boxes. Their arrangement remains rather uniform.

Introduce controlled differences in height, width, binding style, and grouping. Preserve some useful open areas. Use horizontal stacks or a manuscript where the compartment actually suits them.

More importantly, check the complete act of retrieval: approach, finger placement, grip, withdrawal, and replacement. A book can clear a bracket in its stored position yet collide with a curved surround when pulled forwards. The same applies to someone’s hand passing beside scales or horns.

**The rear needs a coherent furniture design.** Finished back panels are a good foundation. The exposed mounting pads and framing still look like technical additions rather than a coordinated rear assembly.

The rear should explain how the cabinet and sculpture are joined, with a consistent system of rails, seats, covers, and access points. It deserves deliberate proportions and terminations. The supplied images offer little reliable rear evidence, so this remains a design task requiring explicit proposals.

“Overbuilding” matters in several different ways here:

| Type | What I see | Better direction |
|---|---|---|
| **Too much ornament** | Dense scales, repeated borders, many grips, and scrolls competing for attention | Concentrate detail around the face, joints, and principal borders; preserve quieter areas |
| **Too much sculptural bulk** | Bodies, feet, tails, and platforms consuming storage or approach space | Evaluate the occupied footprint and book-removal paths |
| **Too many functions** | Reading platform, plant support, drink perch, pet nest, display cabinet, and library combined | Give each added use a clear place and account for its consequences |
| **Too much repetitive modelling effort** | Considerable geometry spent repeating simple motifs while primary anatomy remains weak | Resolve the body, head, wing root, and major sections before refining repetition |
| **Too much presentation lighting** | Several equally bright features drawing attention at once | Establish a focal hierarchy and retain a neutral-light inspection view |

The current performance report does not indicate an urgent need to strip away geometry. The more significant issue is **where the modelling effort has gone**. Repetition is relatively developed; the distinctive anatomical forms remain underdeveloped.

For the existing cabinet, my recommended order is:

1. **Redraw the complete silhouette.** Resolve body fullness, neck bend, head projection, wing sweep, and the spaces between them.
2. **Sculpt the unscaled creature.** Make the torso, throat, skull, jaw, and wing origin convincing in plain clay.
3. **Fit the cabinet to those forms.** Resolve upper compartments, borders, junctions, and retrieval space.
4. **Develop representative details.** Finish one head region, one scale transition, one wing junction, one carved border, and one grip to the intended close-up quality.
5. **Extend those details selectively.** Vary them according to anatomy and furniture function.
6. **Complete the base, rear, materials, and presentation.** Review these from the side and underneath as carefully as from the front.

The next extraction description also needs to specify **how forms change**, alongside what parts exist. For example:

> Describe where the torso reaches its greatest width, where it narrows, how the neck turns, and how the head sits on that turn. Distinguish throat plates from flank and neck scales. Explain where the wing’s supporting fingers originate and how they spread. Describe the transitions between skull, brow, cheek, muzzle, and jaw. Identify which borders belong to cabinet construction and which belong to the creature. State how a person approaches, grips, and removes books around these forms.

That additional information would address the largest gap in the current build: the parts exist and have volume, but their shapes and relationships still need to communicate a convincing dragon and a coherent piece of furniture.

## 2026-10-18T21:01:35.798Z · assistant · final_answer

The eight references offer **eight different relationships between a dragon, a cabinet, and its user**. Their strongest qualities can inform one design, provided we keep those relationships clear.

For the current build, **Image 1 should remain the governing reference**: a usable timber cabinet framed by a green wing and a substantial carved dragon. The other images contribute better anatomy, more purposeful curves, stronger interaction, clearer construction, and more convincing use of space. Their second wings, platforms, chairs, pet beds, and trays belong in a catalogue of alternatives—not automatically in the same cabinet.

Below is the consolidated breakdown, including additions identified by reviewing the images again. Visible features are treated as observations; concealed construction, dimensions, support systems, and fabrication methods remain proposed or unknown.

**1. What each reference contributes to the combined brief.**

| Reference | Defining contribution | Difference from an ordinary bookshelf | What needs restraint or further explanation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Image 1: carved single-wing cabinet** | A disciplined cabinet enclosed by an expressive sculptural silhouette: calm green wing on the left, heavily scaled body and projecting head on the right. | The outer frame becomes a creature, while shelf fronts, uprights, brackets, and feet share a carved language. | Preserve usable compartments. Avoid giving every surface the same ornament density or adding full legs simply because other references have them. |
| **Image 2: two-wing domestic bookcase** | A dragon that appears attentive to the person using its shelves. Its lowered head, central body, planted feet, and small supporting gestures create companionship. | Furniture becomes a character with a relationship to the reader. | The body, feet, mug support, and nest-like lower forms compete with access and circulation. Their usefulness needs testing. |
| **Image 3: circular wall composition** | Strong movement, deliberate voids, and a dragon whose body and tail animate a stable horizontal shelf grid. | The shelving becomes the background for a nearly circular sculptural composition. | A visible book is not necessarily a reachable book. Foreground anatomy can obstruct withdrawal. |
| **Image 4: reading pavilion** | Architectural depth, substantial anatomy, and an occupied space enclosed by wings and tail. | The bookcase becomes a small reading environment with a chair, platform, and spatial boundary. | Entry, chair movement, overhead projections, platform edges, and supporting construction require explicit design. |
| **Image 5: illuminated pavilion** | Light distinguishes membrane, throat plates, shelves, and platform. | Illumination becomes part of the creature’s identity and the display system. | Eye, mouth, throat, wing, shelves, and platform all compete for attention. Strong glow can conceal weak geometry. |
| **Image 6: dragon holding a stepped cabinet** | Clear separation between a complete creature and a recognisably constructed timber cabinet. | The dragon physically engages the furniture through stance, arms, hands, and tail. | The grip must make sense without blocking storage. Stepped compartments need a deliberate capacity plan. |
| **Image 7: shelving wings** | The wing framework genuinely organises the shelves. | Storage follows wing anatomy rather than sitting inside a decorative outline. | High shelves, broad projections, leaning books, and large wing spans introduce access and support questions. |
| **Image 8: standing dragon with sail-like wing** | A restrained, memorable silhouette built around one enormous sweep. | Wing ribs become radial shelf divisions, while the hands provide a small service surface. | Angled compartments need retention and usable book arrangements. The hanging plant adds an unverified load at a projecting tip. |

The common opportunity is to make **every major shape perform several compatible jobs**: establish the silhouette, explain the creature, support the furniture concept, and guide the user’s attention.

---

**2. Overall silhouette: organise the large curves before increasing detail.**

Image 1 succeeds because its two sides have different visual weights. The left wing is broad and relatively quiet, like a stretched green sail held inside warm wooden spars. The right side is dense: overlapping scales gather into a neck, then erupt into a horned head. The cabinet occupies the protected space between them.

That asymmetry is valuable. It gives the eye a place to rest and a place to explore.

Image 3 contributes a different lesson: the dragon’s body and tail create movement across otherwise steady horizontal shelves. Image 8 reduces the idea further, using a single long wing sweep to carry most of the composition. Image 7 spreads the silhouette horizontally, making the wings themselves the storage architecture.

For the current cabinet, establish a hierarchy of curves:

- **Primary curves:** the wing’s outer sweep, the body-to-neck line, and the principal curved cabinet surround.
- **Secondary curves:** wing ribs, jawline, shoulder transitions, scale courses, and the plinth profile.
- **Fine curves:** carving, binding bands, eyelids, grooves, and small scrollwork.

Fine ornament should follow those larger movements. It should not introduce a competing direction every few centimetres.

**Addition from the image review:** negative space needs its own specification. There are three distinct kinds:

1. Space around the outside silhouette.
2. Space between creature and cabinet.
3. Open space within the compartments.

A horn cluster, wing root, and upper cabinet arch can each look attractive independently while collectively closing an important opening. Review the whole model as a flat silhouette and as a simple clay object before judging its surface detail.

---

**3. Body, neck, posture, and expression: establish believable masses beneath the decoration.**

The references repeatedly distinguish **a substantial body from a decorated column**.

In Image 1, the lower body has a broad, pear-like fullness. It narrows through the neck before reaching the head. The scales describe that changing volume; they do not create the entire volume themselves.

Images 4 and 6 make the anatomy easier to understand. The throat occupies one side of the neck, the back another. Shoulders form meaningful masses. Limbs emerge from specific places. Image 7 has weight in its seated thighs and knees. Image 8 has a leg sequence that reads through hip, knee, ankle, and planted toes.

For the current cabinet:

- Give the lower body enough fullness to feel substantial from the side.
- Develop the neck through changing cross-sections, with distinct throat, flank, and dorsal regions.
- Let the neck bend carry the head’s weight and direction.
- Resolve how the body terminates at the plinth.
- Keep the body convincing when every scale and decorative ridge is hidden.

A recognisable creature should already exist at that stage.

**Addition from the image review:** choose an anatomical arrangement deliberately. A dragon with two independent arms and two wings needs understandable shoulder and wing-root relationships. A wing functioning as a transformed forelimb is another valid interpretation. The model should not accidentally alternate between those arrangements.

The emotional posture also needs a decision. Images 1, 4, and 5 favour spectacle and menace. Images 2 and 6 suggest watchfulness and companionship. Image 8 is comparatively restrained.

That choice affects the head angle, brow, jaw opening, tooth exposure, and distance between the face and the reader. It cannot be added through eye colour alone.

---

**4. Head, mouth, eyes, horns, and crest: replace accumulated features with connected anatomy.**

The strongest heads contain a sequence of interlocking forms:

**skull → brow → eye socket → cheek → muzzle → jaw hinge → lower jaw.**

In Image 1, the eye sits inside a much larger arrangement of ridges, cheek forms, horn roots, and nasal structure. The head’s personality comes from those surrounding masses. A large exposed eyeball with decorative tubes placed around it produces a different, more toy-like result.

The head should remain legible from profile, front, rear quarter, and below.

**Skull and muzzle.** Establish the forehead, nasal bridge, cheek width, muzzle taper, and transition into the neck. Avoid a stack of rounded pieces whose intersections are merely hidden by colour.

**Eyes.** Recess the eye within a socket. Shape upper and lower lids with different thicknesses. Let the brow partially shelter the eye. The pupil should belong to a solid insert, while the surrounding anatomy establishes depth in clay mode.

**Nostrils.** Give them a shaped opening and a short internal passage or recessed interior. A dark mark does not explain the thickness of the muzzle.

**Mouth.** An open mouth needs inner cheek walls, palate, gum surfaces, jaw thickness, and a credible hinge region. Its depth should continue into the head rather than end immediately behind the teeth.

**Teeth.** Vary their size and spacing with the jaw. Give them defined roots. Check closure and opposing clearances rather than arranging two independent rows of cones.

**Horns and crest.** Distinguish large horns, smaller dorsal spines, cheek frills, and beard-like projections. These have different roots and directional roles. A few dominant horns should organise the silhouette; smaller forms should support that direction.

Images 4 and 5 demonstrate how glowing mouths exaggerate expression. Image 6 demonstrates how a largely closed mouth can remain characterful through the brow, muzzle, and head inclination.

**Addition from the image review:** define the face’s intended viewing relationship. A head designed to be admired above eye level needs an especially convincing underside. A downward-looking companion needs a readable expression from the position where someone actually selects a book.

---

**5. Wings: decide what kind of construction the wing represents.**

The references contain several distinct wing systems.

| Wing system | References | Required design logic |
|---|---|---|
| **Framed membrane beside a cabinet** | Especially Image 1 | A substantial perimeter, internal ribs, green infill, and a coherent relationship to the cabinet. |
| **Shelving enclosed within a wing outline** | Images 2 and 3 | The wing provides the silhouette while a more conventional shelf arrangement occupies its interior. |
| **Wing as an enclosing architectural surface** | Images 4 and 5 | Greater depth, an understandable root, and a relationship to the occupied reading space. |
| **Wing framework becoming the shelving** | Images 7 and 8 | Ribs, divisions, shelves, trailing edges, and storage compartments must be designed together. |
| **Membrane wing beside a separate cabinet** | Image 6 | The creature and furniture remain distinct, with a wing that still reads as part of the animal. |

For Image 1’s direction, the wing needs more than a curved outline and several parallel ribs.

Its leading edge should have a recognisable structural emphasis. Internal ribs should fan from a plausible root and change section as they travel. The upper curl should conclude the main sweep. The trailing edge should have controlled scallops and terminations.

The green panels should show slight spatial shaping: camber, tension, or shallow curvature. From the side, they should possess a finished edge and an intentional relationship to the ribs.

**Addition from the image review:** choose between a continuous membrane with ribs seated against it and separately retained panels between ribs. Both are possible interpretations. They produce different seams, rear appearances, and attachment details.

The current proposed **8 mm panel thickness** can remain a study assumption for a carved or fabricated cabinet wing. It should not be treated as an anatomical measurement derived from the images.

Also distinguish visual warmth from actual translucency. The amber appearance in Image 5 does not establish how light would physically pass through the material.

---

**6. Scales, belly plates, skin, and carved surfaces: use regional anatomy rather than uniform repetition.**

The references do not use one scale pattern everywhere.

Images 2, 4, 5, 6, and 7 distinguish broad ventral plates from smaller flank scales. Image 1 uses larger, rounded plates over the lower body and tighter arrangements around the neck. Image 6 adds a fine pebbled surface between larger anatomical forms.

These differences are essential to recognition.

A useful surface map would separate:

- **Throat and belly:** broad overlapping scutes following the neck and torso.
- **Flanks:** smaller scales wrapping the body’s cross-section.
- **Shoulders and hips:** transitions around large joints and changing curvature.
- **Face:** finer plates and ridges that preserve expression.
- **Dorsal line:** crest elements and directional spines.
- **Flexible transitions:** restrained folds or smaller surface units where movement is implied.

Scale courses should bend around the body. Their width, overlap, orientation, and spacing should respond to curvature.

For separately modelled plates, establish a seated root, a supported middle, and a controlled lifted tip. Check visible undersides and neighbouring clearance. Random rotation is not a substitute for anatomical placement.

**Important consistency correction:** the images do not prove that their scales are separately attached plates. Some could represent relief carved into a continuous timber mass. The current proposed **3–5 mm individual plates** describe one modelling interpretation, not a requirement applicable to every reference.

Choose the construction story first:

- Continuous carved relief.
- Attached timber plates.
- A sculpted creature with skin and scutes.
- A deliberate hybrid with clearly defined transitions.

That choice affects every edge, seam, underside, and material response.

---

**7. Cabinet topology: describe the actual storage arrangement, including difficult upper compartments.**

“Five levels and three bays” is a useful starting description, but it does not fully define a cabinet.

The images offer several storage topologies:

- Image 1: mostly regular bays within a curved surround.
- Image 2: two shelving wings divided by a central creature.
- Image 3: horizontal shelving behind a foreground sculptural composition.
- Images 4 and 5: a deep, enclosing shelving wall.
- Image 6: stepped and interlocking rectangular compartments.
- Image 7: upper wing storage combined with a lower conventional cabinet.
- Image 8: radial and diagonal compartments following a sail-like wing.

For the current build, retain the orderly cabinet core from Image 1. Then explicitly describe every place where the surround changes usable space.

That includes:

- The width and height of each compartment.
- Clear depth after backing, trim, and projections.
- Upper compartments beneath curves.
- Shelf-to-divider junctions.
- End-panel and backing relationships.
- Support beneath shelf ends.
- Corner returns and top terminations.
- Areas intentionally reserved for display rather than books.

A small wedge near a wing tip may hold a horizontal volume or decorative object. It should not be populated with upright books that cannot physically fit or be removed.

**Addition from the image review:** specify the cabinet in plan and section as well as elevation. A convincing front view can conceal a shallow storage cavity, an excessively deep dragon body, or a head that projects far beyond the base.

The existing **1.90 m width, 2.10 m height, approximately 500 mm overall depth, 300 mm usable shelf depth, 28 mm shelves, and 12 mm backing** remain prototype assumptions. The overall envelope must be recalculated from the actual projections rather than maintained only as a label.

---

**8. Books, display objects, and access: distinguish storage clearance from use clearance.**

The books are a major scale reference. They also reveal whether the furniture is genuinely usable.

Image 1 mixes upright books, small stacks, restrained leaning groups, and a manuscript roll. Images 4–6 combine books with display objects. Images 7 and 8 use more irregular and angled arrangements.

Those variations should look selected, not randomly scattered.

Each book still needs cover boards, a recessed paper mass, spine form, hinge detail, and plausible binding variation. Stacked books need believable contact. Leaning books need actual support. A manuscript roll needs a coiled paper section rather than a decorative cylinder.

The larger improvement is operational:

**A book can fit in a compartment and still be impossible to retrieve.**

Test the space needed to:

- Place fingers around the spine.
- Lift the book slightly.
- Tilt its upper edge.
- Withdraw it past a bracket, rib, claw, or curved surround.
- Return it without catching neighbouring objects.

Image 3 is particularly useful here: the foreground dragon creates attractive layering while potentially blocking shelves behind it. Image 6 poses the same question at its gripping hand.

Angled shelves in Images 7 and 8 need intentional retention and support. Their books should respond to gravity.

**Addition from the image review:** include useful empty space. An unfilled compartment can preserve access, display the cabinet’s construction, and prevent the entire composition becoming a wall of equally dense detail.

---

**9. Carved borders, scrollwork, and gripping brackets: make ornament belong to its support.**

Image 1 has a coherent ornamental language across shelf fronts, uprights, curved borders, grip motifs, and feet. Images 4 and 5 develop larger flowing ornament around the shoulder and wing root. Image 8 achieves character with comparatively broad carved forms and less surface saturation.

The current design should distinguish **carved relief** from **cords laid over a surface**.

A carved ribbon can widen, flatten, rise, sink, and disappear into a surrounding field. Its background may be recessed. An interlace crossing needs a deliberate over-and-under relationship. A scroll should have an origin, a controlled turn, and a resolved termination.

At cabinet junctions:

- Motifs should stop or turn intentionally.
- Relief should remain supported by the border beneath it.
- Carving should not pass through an adjacent board.
- Small decorative patches should not conceal an unresolved structural meeting.

Grip-like brackets deserve similar attention. Three identical rounded tabs on a block do not communicate the same thing as a hand or claw wrapping a shelf edge.

Use unequal digits, knuckle transitions, separated grooves, and a visible return beneath or around the furniture. Establish whether the motif merely decorates the joint or appears to support it.

**Addition from the image review:** do not apply gripping motifs at every junction by default. A few deliberate contacts can make the dragon seem engaged with the cabinet. Uniform repetition makes the same features read as standard hardware.

---

**10. Plinth, feet, tail, and floor contact: explain how the composition meets the ground.**

The references have very different grounding strategies.

Image 1 uses a low moulded plinth and scroll feet. Image 2 combines planted feet with lower nest-like forms. Image 3 uses a sweeping tail as a visual base. Images 4 and 5 stand on circular platforms. Image 6 uses large feet and a tail around a cabinet. Image 7 sits the creature above a lower bookcase. Image 8 stands the dragon on articulated legs.

These are alternatives with different consequences.

For the current cabinet, the most coherent solution remains a resolved timber plinth:

- A deliberate moulding section.
- Continuous corner returns.
- Defined cabinet bearing surfaces.
- A finished underside.
- Scroll feet that remain readable beneath the base.
- A controlled transition where the dragon body arrives at the plinth.

Adding more stacked layers will not automatically improve it. The sections need different roles and proportions.

A tail can strengthen continuity, especially as in Image 3, but it should be introduced only if its route, attachment, clearance, and contribution to the silhouette are clear.

**Addition from the image review:** apparent visual balance is not proof of stability. Image 8’s tail and feet suggest a balanced pose, but the image does not reveal the internal support or anchoring.

Where a real construction proposal is intended, floor contact, mounting, and load paths need separate development. The rendered pose alone cannot establish them.

---

**11. Materials and edges: give the object a consistent fabrication language.**

Image 1 reads as carved brown timber with contrasting green infill. Image 6 strongly separates warm wooden furniture from a pale-bellied, dark-skinned creature. Image 7 carries a more unified timber language across dragon and shelving. Image 8 uses broad sculpted wood forms and visible grain direction to support its restrained silhouette.

For the current build, the central question is:

**What would a person believe this object was made from when standing beside it?**

If it is assembled carved timber, grain direction, seams, plate attachment, and board orientation should support that interpretation. If a feature represents a continuous carved mass, its material should not break into unrelated grain directions without explanation.

Different regions also need different edge behaviour:

- Broad body masses: flowing transitions.
- Shelf edges: small, controlled easing or a defined profile.
- Scale rims: thin but tangible.
- Carved ridges: selectively crisp.
- Horn roots: substantial transitions.
- Horn tips: controlled taper.
- Recesses: depth and material response rather than black paint alone.

Uniform rounding makes everything resemble moulded plastic. Uniform sharpness makes everything resemble unfinished machining.

**Addition from the image review:** match surface-detail scale to furniture scale. Fine skin should remain fine. Wood pores should not become trenches. Page fibres should not read as thick ropes when the camera approaches.

At 5–10 cm, the convincing details are often modest: cover overhang, a fitted seam, a shallow recess, a rounded rim, a fastener seat, or the narrow shadow beneath a contact.

---

**12. Lighting: assign a job to every source.**

Image 3 uses illuminated shelves to separate books from dark sculpture. Image 4 combines room daylight with accent lighting. Image 5 demonstrates how quickly emphasis becomes competition. Images 7 and 8 show how much readable form can be achieved without extensive self-illumination.

For the cabinet, light should reveal:

- The body’s changing volume.
- Separation between scales.
- Wing camber and rib sections.
- Mouth depth.
- Carved relief.
- Shelf contents.
- Contact with the base.

A glowing eye can be a small focal point. A mouth light can reveal its interior. Shelf lighting can assist use. These purposes should have a hierarchy.

If the eye, teeth, throat, wing, every shelf, and plinth all demand attention, the cabinet loses its focal structure.

**Addition from the image review:** specify the physical accommodation of lighting where it is part of the design. Fixtures need housings or recesses, concealed routes, and access for servicing. Those are proposed construction details, since the images do not establish them.

Also preserve the distinction between emitted light, reflected light, and translucency. Warmly lit green or amber material should not automatically become an emissive surface.

Clay and grazing-light views remain essential because they expose the geometry beneath the atmosphere.

---

**13. Human use, accessories, and architectural depth: carry forward the purpose without importing every object.**

The domestic and pavilion references add value because they show people doing things around the dragon.

Image 2 shows a person selecting a book. Images 4 and 5 suggest a seated reading experience. Image 6 presents a creature holding furniture. Image 8 turns hands into a small tray.

These interactions deserve explicit design envelopes.

For a cabinet, test standing approach, reaching, book withdrawal, and movement past projecting features. For a pavilion, add chair pull-out, reclining space, entry through the tail boundary, seated headroom, platform access, and rug-to-platform relationships.

The images also contain accessories that should be classified carefully:

| Accessory or contextual feature | Useful idea | Further requirement if adopted |
|---|---|---|
| **Mug support in Image 2** | The creature can offer a practical gesture. | A usable, cleanable surface that does not obstruct books. |
| **Nest-like pet area in Image 2** | The furniture can support companionship beyond reading. | Defined access, removable cushion, cleaning access, and separation from storage. |
| **Chair and rug in Images 4–5** | Establish an occupied reading place. | Real movement space and physically constructed edges if modelled closely. |
| **Plant on the head in Image 7** | A playful accessory. | It remains an accessory, not an anatomical feature or automatic requirement. |
| **Hanging plant in Image 8** | Extends the silhouette and adds domestic life. | A separately considered attachment and water-management arrangement. |
| **Books and mug on Image 8’s hands** | Makes the dragon participate in use. | Stable support, sufficient surface area, and an intentional relationship to the user. |

These additions should earn their space. Combining all of them would create competing uses and a much larger object.

The room itself also contributes presentation effects. Image 8’s cast shadows strengthen the silhouette; they are consequences of lighting and room geometry, not additional carved features.

---

**14. Rear construction, attachments, and maintenance: develop what the images cannot show.**

Most of the evidence is strongly front-facing. Rear and underside construction remain largely unknown.

That uncertainty should become a design task rather than an invitation to improvise invisible geometry.

The cabinet needs:

- Consistent backing and panel seams.
- Defined rebates or retaining arrangements.
- Rear surfaces finished to the intended inspection standard.
- Wing and body attachment zones.
- Accessible assembly points.
- Clear relationships between decorative assemblies and the cabinet core.
- Underside construction that explains the feet and plinth.

The wing needs a rear face with deliberate ribs, seams, or retaining details. The dragon needs finished rear contours rather than a front sculpture abruptly flattened against the backing.

**Addition from the image review:** include assembly order and access. A beautiful attachment detail is ineffective if another component makes its fastener unreachable.

For a future fabricated version, modularity, transport, wall clearance, and repairability would also affect the design. These should be labelled proposed requirements, not presented as construction methods observed in the images.

---

**15. Where “more detail” becomes overbuilding.**

The images demonstrate several different forms of excess. They require different remedies.

| Type of overbuilding | How it appears | Better quality decision |
|---|---|---|
| **Ornamental saturation** | Scales, carving, scrolls, horns, and borders all have equal density. | Concentrate detail around focal anatomy and meaningful junctions; preserve quiet fields. |
| **Excessive bulk** | Thick surrounds and anatomy consume usable compartments or project far into the room. | Check the storage and circulation envelope alongside the silhouette. |
| **Multifunction clutter** | Tray, plant hanger, pet nest, chair enclosure, and display cabinet accumulate together. | Select uses that support the main purpose. |
| **Lighting saturation** | Every feature glows. | Establish a primary focal source and subordinate task or accent lighting. |
| **Repetition mistaken for refinement** | Hundreds of identical scales or ornaments cover unresolved masses. | Resolve a representative region carefully before repeating it. |
| **Construction ambiguity** | Ribs, hands, borders, shelves, and anatomy merge without clear interfaces. | Design contacts and joints before decorating them. |
| **Computational excess** | Fine geometry is distributed everywhere regardless of visibility. | Retain physical construction while reserving finer geometry for meaningful inspection. |

The principal quality problem is often **where effort is concentrated**, rather than the total amount of effort.

A carefully shaped cheek, convincing wing root, and resolved cabinet corner can improve the object more than another hundred decorative plates.

---

**16. Quality consistency: use separate tests for separate claims.**

The combined brief needs more than a general “high quality” requirement.

| Review | What it establishes |
|---|---|
| **Silhouette review** | The dragon and cabinet read clearly at room distance. |
| **Plain-mass clay review** | Anatomy and furniture remain convincing without scales, colour, grain, or glow. |
| **Solid-geometry audit** | Intended solids have valid closed construction, thickness, winding, and finite geometry. |
| **Placement audit** | Books, shelves, brackets, scales, teeth, ribs, and attachments have appropriate relationships. |
| **Use review** | People can approach, reach, retrieve, replace, sit, or move as intended. |
| **Close-up visual review** | Edges, layers, contacts, cavities, and transitions remain believable at 5–10 cm. |
| **Rear and underside review** | Quality continues beyond the presentation face. |
| **Performance review** | Navigation remains responsive in the actual viewing configuration. |

These checks cannot substitute for one another.

A closed mesh can still have an unconvincing head. Two watertight objects can still intersect incorrectly. A collision-free book can still be inaccessible to a hand. A beautiful screenshot can still conceal an unfinished rear face.

Attachment contacts also need local definitions. A scale may intentionally contact the body at its root; that does not justify arbitrary penetration elsewhere. The same principle applies to horn roots, teeth, gripping fingers, wing ribs, and feet.

For dimension controls, review the endpoint combinations as well as the default. Changing shelf depth or head projection must not leave the books, brackets, or attachments in their previous positions.

---

**17. What the renewed image review adds to the extraction description.**

The most significant omissions are more specific than a request for additional detail:

- **Curve intent:** where each major curve begins, changes direction, thickens, and terminates.
- **Regional anatomy:** the distinction between belly scutes, flank scales, flexible transitions, facial plates, and dorsal crest.
- **Wing type:** membrane, framed infill, shelving enclosure, shelving framework, or architectural enclosure.
- **Pose and gaze:** how the dragon relates to a standing or seated user.
- **Storage topology:** actual compartment shapes, difficult upper wedges, and display-only areas.
- **Operational clearance:** the movement needed to remove a book or use a chair.
- **Contact logic:** where a hand grips, a plate seats, a rib bears, or a body meets the base.
- **Fabrication language:** continuous carving, attached pieces, sculpted anatomy, or an explicitly defined hybrid.
- **Quiet areas:** surfaces and voids intentionally left less detailed.
- **Rear access and assembly order:** how unseen construction could be completed and maintained.
- **Context classification:** which objects belong to the furniture, which are accessories, and which belong only to the photographed room.
- **Evidence limits:** which dimensions, materials, attachments, and structural assumptions remain unknown.

Watermarks, logos, contact details, and attribution text remain source-image information. They are not furniture features.

---

**18. A consolidated extraction prompt that incorporates these findings.**

> Describe the dragon bookcase as a complete spatial object and a usable piece of furniture. Identify its design type and separate defining features from optional accessories and room context.
>
> Establish the overall silhouette, principal curves, pose, gaze, proportions, projections, and important negative spaces. Explain how the dragon and cabinet relate: framing, holding, enclosing, sitting upon, or becoming the shelving.
>
> For every major component, describe the front, side, rear, underside, thickness, edge profile, layers, material interpretation, attachments, support, contact, and clearance. State what changes as the viewer moves around it.
>
> Describe anatomy through underlying masses and cross-sections before surface decoration. Distinguish skull, muzzle, jaw, throat, shoulder, wing root, torso, limbs, and tail where present. Describe regional scales and plates rather than applying one repeated pattern everywhere.
>
> Specify the wing’s construction type, root, leading edge, ribs, panel or membrane shaping, trailing edge, tip, and rear construction. Explain whether shelves occupy the wing or form part of its framework.
>
> Define the cabinet’s compartment topology and usable dimensions, including curved upper clearances, backing, supports, shelf junctions, trim, and retrieval paths. Distinguish book storage from display compartments.
>
> Explain carving depth, motif origins and terminations, interlace crossings, bracket grips, and the supporting material beneath relief.
>
> Identify geometry required for silhouette, mass, cavities, thickness, separation, and contact. Reserve surface maps for fine grain, pores, fibres, and print. Set the intended inspection distance, including 5–10 cm where required.
>
> Label information as text-derived, image-derived, estimated, proposed, or unknown. Do not infer concealed construction or structural adequacy from appearance alone.
>
> For each component record: **source evidence → description → modelling method → attachment and clearance → verification → remaining uncertainty**.
>
> State the detail hierarchy, intended quiet areas, lighting hierarchy, assembly access, and tests needed to preserve quality when dimensions change.

For the current cabinet, the improvement sequence should be: **resolve the silhouette and body masses; reconstruct the head; develop the wing root, ribs, and panels; settle cabinet junctions and retrieval clearances; refine representative scales, carving, and grips; finish the base and rear; then tune materials and lighting.** This carries the strongest lessons from all eight references into the existing direction without losing the clarity that makes Image 1 the appropriate foundation.

## 2026-10-18T21:20:57.512Z · assistant · final_answer

<proposed_plan>
# Dragon bookshelf showroom and reusable design library

## 1. Summary and boundaries

Create an independent Three.js showroom containing **eight distinct dragon bookshelf designs**, supported by a reusable component library and a visual workbench.

- Build in `dragon-bookshelf-showroom` and `dragon-design-library`. Preserve the existing studies unchanged.
- Serve the showroom at **http://127.0.0.1:4192/** using the locally installed Three.js version.
- Preserve each reference’s individual pose, proportions, wing construction, materials, and lighting character.
- Include **dragons, cabinets, books, integrated lighting, and integral bases or platforms**. Exclude chairs, rugs, plants, people, pets, cushions, drinks, and unrelated decorative props.
- Prioritise silhouette, bodily mass, curvature, wing shape, wingspan, and convincing construction during inspection from **5–10 cm**.
- Treat all dimensions, concealed attachments, and fabrication choices as proposed construction unless supported by visible evidence. This remains a digital design study rather than fabrication certification.

## 2. Showroom and eight exhibits

### Showroom arrangement

Build a restrained **24 × 40 × 6 m** gallery with a solid floor, neutral walls, controlled lighting, and eight exhibition stations.

- Arrange four stations on each side of a central aisle, facing inward.
- Provide space behind and beside each exhibit for inspection. Keep sculptural projections within their station.
- Present every design at its proposed physical size; do not normalise all eight to the same height.
- Use a minimap and numbered exhibit selector for navigation.
- Provide **Walk, Inspect, and Free flight** modes. Walk includes collision and a 1.65 m standing eye height; Inspect supports object-centred orbit, physical dolly, pan, and underside views.
- Entering detailed inspection preserves a return position in the gallery. Material and lighting changes preserve the camera.
- Keep showroom architecture visually subordinate to the collection.

### Exhibit definitions

The following envelopes are **proposed starting dimensions**, not measurements extracted from the images. Display measured generated bounds alongside these assumptions.

| Exhibit | Proposed envelope W × H × D | Required identity and construction |
|---|---:|---|
| **01 — Carved single-wing cabinet** | 1.90 × 2.10 × 0.55 m | Green wing left; broad scaled body right; narrowing, bent neck; outward-facing horned head; regular carved shelving; selected gripping brackets; scroll-footed plinth. Preserve asymmetry and quiet membrane areas. |
| **02 — Two-wing domestic bookcase** | 3.20 × 2.80 × 0.90 m | Central attentive dragon, downward-inclined head, two tall shelving wings, broad belly plates, smaller flank scales, substantial feet and lower curved forms. Retain sculpted supporting gestures without accessories. |
| **03 — Circular wall composition** | 3.40 × 3.20 × 0.75 m | Dark dragon moving across horizontal shelves, enclosing wing curves, strong negative spaces, and a tail sweeping along the bottom and outward. Include a finished mounting/backing assembly for rear inspection. |
| **04 — Reading pavilion structure** | 4.80 × 3.80 × 2.80 m | Substantial dragon at left, enclosing membrane wing, deep shelving wing at right, pronounced neck curvature, shoulder/wing-root transitions, tail boundary, and integral platform. Leave the occupied centre empty. |
| **05 — Illuminated pavilion structure** | 4.80 × 3.80 × 2.80 m | Preserve the separate membrane, shelving wing, throat plates, carved shoulder, and platform. Provide reference-character lighting and a restrained alternative using physical light sources and housings. |
| **06 — Dragon holding a stepped cabinet** | 2.60 × 3.20 × 0.95 m | Complete creature distinct from its timber cabinet: inclined head, shoulders, gripping arm and hand, hips, legs, feet, wrapping tail, pale belly plates, fine dark flank detail, and pale ribbed wing. |
| **07 — Shelving wings** | 4.40 × 3.60 × 1.00 m | Seated central dragon above a lower cabinet; wide wings whose ribs genuinely organise shelves; scalloped lower edges; substantial thighs, knees, hands and feet; tail descending beside the lower cabinet. |
| **08 — Standing dragon with sail wing** | 2.20 × 3.20 × 0.95 m | Slender standing creature, articulated legs and planted toes, left-facing head, large upper-right wing sweep, radial compartments, broad carved anatomical forms, and supporting hands holding books. |

Each exhibit receives its own reference image, silhouette specification, anatomical proportions, storage topology, construction interpretation, and evidence record. None may be implemented solely by recolouring or stretching Exhibit 01.

## 3. Component library and visual workbench

### Library architecture

Separate reusable geometry, anatomy, furniture, ornament, materials, placement, and exhibit recipes.

Provide these public interfaces:

- `createDragonComponent(THREE, type, spec, context)` — build an individual reusable component.
- `createDragonAssembly(THREE, spec)` — assemble a complete dragon bookcase from an exhibit recipe.
- `createDragonShowroom(THREE, root, options)` — mount the showroom and workbench.
- Export a component catalogue and eight immutable reference presets.

An assembly returns its scene root, part registry, attachment relationships, inspection anchors, storage envelopes, collision geometry, measured bounds, and disposal method.

Every part retains stable identifiers, component ownership, material assignment, and evidence confidence through batching and detail changes. Attachment records identify **specific parts and local contact regions**.

### Component families

| Family | Required reusable capabilities |
|---|---|
| **Body and posture** | Curved centreline lofts, changing cross-sections, neck/throat regions, shoulders, torso, haunches, seated and standing arrangements. |
| **Head** | Configurable skull, muzzle, brow, cheek, jaw, mouth cavity, eye sockets, nostrils, and expression. Produce connected anatomical masses rather than visibly intersecting primitives. |
| **Features** | Rooted horns, crest spines, cheek frills, eyelids, eye inserts, teeth, and jaw details with controlled taper and attachment. |
| **Limbs and tail** | Articulated arms, gripping hands, thighs, knees, ankles, planted feet, and curved tails with finished undersides. |
| **Wings** | Framed membrane, enclosing membrane, shelving enclosure, shelf-bearing framework, and radial sail configurations. Support handedness and independent wing poses. |
| **Surface regions** | Broad ventral scutes, smaller flank scales, facial plates, dorsal elements, and continuous carved relief. Support attached plates and relief as distinct construction methods. |
| **Furniture** | Regular bays, stepped cubbies, curved shelving, wing shelves, radial compartments, backing, supports, plinths, feet, and platforms. |
| **Ornament** | Profiled borders, flattened carved ribbons, scrolls, genuine over/under crossings, and anatomically shaped grip motifs. |
| **Books and lighting** | Complete bindings and paper volumes; supported book arrangements; integrated light housings, lenses or diffusers, and light sources. |

Reuse proven mathematical and audit helpers from the existing study through a separately maintained library implementation. Do not inherit its fixed anatomy or cabinet layout.

### Workbench behaviour

Provide a searchable component catalogue, isolated component preview, assembly tree, and selected-part inspector.

- Expose guided controls for overall height, cabinet width, usable depth, wingspan, wing sweep, camber, tip curl, rib spread, body fullness, neck curvature, head projection, and head proportions.
- Show only controls supported by the selected component or recipe.
- Support compatible component substitution through declared attachment slots. Explain incompatible selections without creating broken assemblies.
- Recalculate connected geometry, storage capacity, attachments, bounds, and collisions after changes.
- Keep board, paper, and scale thickness physical; do not stretch books with the surrounding cabinet.
- Save edited assemblies as named variants without overwriting reference presets.
- Support versioned JSON export/import containing the recipe, dimensions, component choices, materials, seed, and evidence metadata.
- Provide reset, undo/redo, complete-component isolation, explosion, and front/rear/side/overhead/underside views.

Display overall bounds, cabinet dimensions, left/right wing spans, and maximum projection using optional dimension guides. Define wingspan consistently as the horizontal projection between wing extremities; show root-to-tip reach separately for individual wings.

Direct mesh sculpting, arbitrary curve-point editing, and unrestricted drag-and-drop assembly are outside this version.

## 4. Geometry, rendering, and quality controls

### Construction requirements

- Build anatomy from custom cross-sections, lofts, shaped sweeps, and continuous sculpted volumes. Use subtraction for genuine cavities.
- Give membranes, scales, books, shelves, backing, platforms, and lighting housings closed construction and physical thickness.
- Develop wing roots, leading edges, rib fans, scalloped trailing edges, camber, and tips individually for each wing type.
- Make scales follow anatomical regions and changing curvature. Resolve their roots, overlap clearance, rims, and undersides.
- Construct cabinet interfaces before decoration and book placement. Resolve shelf ends, dividers, curved boundaries, back panels, and moulding returns.
- Distinguish stored-book clearance from retrieval clearance. Keep hands, brackets, ribs, and anatomy outside withdrawal paths.
- Finish rear and underside construction with the same deliberate treatment as the presentation face.

Use 28 mm shelf boards, 12 mm backing, 8 mm fabricated membrane panels, and approximately 4 mm attached scales as initial component defaults where applicable. Continuous carved relief uses its own depth specification.

### Appearance and inspection

Provide reference materials, clay, neutral lighting, and grazing lighting. Integrated exhibit illumination remains independently adjustable.

Use geometry for silhouette, mass, cavities, edge profiles, relief, layers, and contact. Use surface maps for grain, fibres, pores, and print.

Each exhibit receives bookmarks for its head, mouth, eye, wing root, wing edge, rib junction, regional scales, shelf junction, book edge, base, rear, and underside where applicable.

### Performance strategy

- Generate geometry in workers and cache reusable components.
- Use complete lower-detail solids for distant exhibits.
- Load fine detail around the active exhibit and selected components; pin selected detail during inspection.
- Preserve part selection through batching and instancing.
- Update static shadows when geometry or lighting changes.
- Dispose of replaced geometry and unused high-detail assets.

Target **at least 30 fps at a 1920 × 1080 render buffer** during gallery navigation and focused inspection. Report actual performance, loading times, draw calls, triangle counts, and measurement conditions.

### Visual gates

Complete each exhibit through these gates:

1. Reference silhouette and negative space.
2. Plain anatomical masses and furniture proportions.
3. Construction, attachments, and usable compartments.
4. Representative close-up details before repetition.
5. Rear/underside completion, materials, and lighting.

Reject toy-like facial proportions, canopy-like parallel wing ribs, uniform shingle scales, unresolved primitive intersections, floating decoration, and texture-dependent cavities.

## 5. Verification and deliverables

### Automated and visual acceptance

- Audit every intended solid for finite coordinates, closed boundaries, consistent winding, positive volume, and non-degenerate construction.
- Run a separate placement audit using **0.2 mm tolerance**, broad-phase bounds, triangle intersection checks, and containment checks.
- Validate books against books, cabinet, brackets, hands, wings, and anatomy; scales against neighbouring scales and body; ribs against panels; teeth against jaws; horn roots against heads; and bases against the floor.
- Allow attachment penetration only within declared local contact regions.
- Test all eight defaults, every supported control endpoint, pairwise boundary combinations, and complete endpoint combinations within each dimensional control group. Report coverage explicitly.
- Verify walk collisions, exhibit transitions, physical dolly, pan, flight, touch gestures, fullscreen, saved cameras, reload persistence, JSON round trips, compatible substitutions, undo/redo, and narrow layouts.
- Verify geometry changes preserve unrelated camera and lighting state and do not leak resources.
- Capture each exhibit from front, rear, side, overhead, and underside, plus representative 5–10 cm clay and grazing-light close-ups.
- Compare every exhibit directly with its source image for posture, curvature, proportions, wing shape, wingspan, negative space, and storage arrangement.
- Keep topology results, placement results, usability checks, and visual judgement separate.

### Delivery

Provide the running showroom, reusable source library, eight presets, workbench, component/API documentation, preserved reference evidence, updated extraction prompt, screenshots, and verification report.

For every major component, document:

**description → source evidence → modelling method → attachment/support → clearance → verification → remaining uncertainty.**

The existing single-wing study remains available at port 4191; the new collection and its workbench run independently at port 4192.
</proposed_plan>
